ChatGPT and post-test probability
Samuel J. Weisenthal samuel_weisenthal@urmc.rochester.edu

Abstract




1
Abstract

Reinforcement learning-based large language models, such as ChatGPT, are believed to have potential to aid human experts in many domains, including healthcare. There is, however, little work on ChatGPT’s ability to perform a key task in healthcare: formal, probabilistic medical diagnostic reasoning. This type of reasoning is used, for example, to update a pre-test probability to a post-test probability. In this work, we probe ChatGPT’s ability to perform this task. In particular, we ask ChatGPT to give examples of how to use Bayes rule for medical diagnosis. Our prompts range from queries that use terminology from pure probability (e.g., requests for a posterior of A given B and C) to queries that use terminology from medical diagnosis (e.g., requests for a posterior probability of Covid given a test result and cough). We show how the introduction of medical variable names leads to an increase in the number of errors that ChatGPT makes. Given our results, we also show how one can use prompt engineering to facilitate ChatGPT’s partial avoidance of these errors. We discuss our results in light of recent commentaries on sensitivity and specificity. We also discuss how our results might inform new research directions for large language models.

2
Introduction

In concert with reinforcement learning techniques (e.g.,
Schulman et al. 2017
), large language models (e.g.,
(
Radford et al. 2018
;
Brown et al. 2020
)
) have led to impressive, human-like chat capabilities. Systems such as ChatGPT
(
OpenAI 2023
)
are believed to have potential to aid human experts in many specialized domains, including medicine and public health
(
Khan et al. 2023
;
Biswas 2023
)
. This enthusiasm is based partly on reports that ChatGPT has shown good performance on certain medically-relevant tasks, such as medical licensing exams
(
Kumah-Crystal et al. 2023
;
Fijačko et al. 2023
;
Kung et al. 2023
;
Ayers et al. 2023
;
Goodman et al. 2023
)
.
Indeed, large language models have excellent ability to access information, which might help them answer medical questions and even generate differential diagnoses
(
Kanjee et al. 2023
;
Liu et al. 2023
)
. However, medical diagnosis also requires reasoning. Although systems like ChatGPT can emulate reasoning, authors have criticized its true deductive reasoning capabilities
(
Shakarian et al. 2023
;
Borji 2023
;
Liévin et al. 2022
;
Wang et al. 2023
;
Bang et al. 2023
)
. To our knowledge, no study has investigated ChatGPT’s ability to perform formal, probabilistic medical diagnostic reasoning. Probabilistic medical reasoning is used, for example, to update a pre-test probability to a post-test probability.

In this work, we probe ChatGPT’s insight into probabilistic medical diagnostic reasoning by asking it to devise examples in which Bayes rule is used to obtain a post-test probability. In particular, we give it a challenging clinical scenario in which not only the test, but also the patient symptoms, must be taken into account. We discuss the errors that we observe in the responses of ChatGPT in the light of certain commentaries, such as those by
Moons et al. 1997
,
Harrell et al. 2001
,
Moons and Harrell 2003
, and
Guggenmoos-Holzmann and van Houwelingen 2000
, on the assumption that sensitivity is a constant characteristic of the test, and therefore does not depend on patient covariates.
We also explore prompt engineering approaches in order to improve ChatGPT’s ability to perform probabilistic diagnostic reasoning, and we briefly discuss the implications of our results on new research directions for large language models.

3
Background

3.1
Diagnostic problem

In the standard diagnostic problem, if
dz+
is a binary random variable representing disease presence and
test+
is a binary random variable representing a positive test result, we have that

\displaystyle p(dz+|test+)

\displaystyle=\frac{p(test+|dz+)p(dz+)}{p(test+)}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\text{sensitivity})(\text{pre-test probability})}{p(test+)},

(1)

where sensitivity is
p(test+|dz+)
and pre-test probability is
p(dz+)
. Using the law of total probability, the denominator can be further written as

p(test+)=(\text{sensitivity})(\text{pre-test probability})+(1-\text{specificity})(1-(\text{pre-test probability})).

In this work, since we will be assessing the ability of ChatGPT to perform probabilistic diagnostic reasoning, we will also condition on the presence of a symptom, cough. The symptom, cough, is also known as a patient covariate. This is different from the standard medical diagnosis problems, shown in (
3.1
), which often only consider the case where when one conditions on a test. For example expositions of the prevailing framework, see, e.g.,
Ledley and Lusted 1959
,
Parikh et al. 2008
, and
Trevethan 2017
. In particular, conditioning on the covariate cough makes this a more difficult diagnostic reasoning problem, as we will now describe.

If we let
cough
be a binary random variable, we obtain the post-test probability

\displaystyle p(dz+|test+,cough)

\displaystyle=\frac{p(test+|dz+,cough)p(dz+|cough)}{p(test+|cough)}

(2)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\text{sensitivity})(\text{pre-test probability})}{p(test+|cough)}.

Note that the denominator can be rewritten

\displaystyle p(test+|cough)

\displaystyle=(\text{sensitivity})(\text{pre-test probability})

\displaystyle\ \ \ +(1-\text{specificity})(1-(\text{pre-test probability})).

One might also write

\displaystyle p(dz+|test+,cough)

\displaystyle=\frac{p(test+,cough|dz+)p(dz+)}{p(test+,cough)}

\displaystyle=\frac{p(test+|cough,dz+)p(cough|dz+)p(dz+)}{p(test+,cough)}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\text{sensitivity})p(cough|dz+)(\text{pre-test probability})}{p(test+,cough)}.

(3)

Note that, as for (
2
), the denominator in (
3.1
) can be rewritten

\displaystyle p(test+,cough)

\displaystyle=p(test+,dz+,cough)+p(test+,dz-,cough)

\displaystyle=p(test+,cough|dz+)p(dz+)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +p(test+,cough|dz-)p(dz-)

\displaystyle=p(test+|cough,dz+)p(cough|dz+)p(dz+)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +p(test+|cough,dz-)p(cough|dz-)p(dz-)

\displaystyle=p(test+|cough,dz+)p(cough|dz+)p(dz+)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +(1-p(test-|cough,dz-))p(cough|dz-)(1-p(dz+))

\displaystyle=(\text{sensitivity})p(cough|dz+)(\text{pre-test probability})

\displaystyle\ \ \ +(1-\text{specificity})p(cough|dz-)(1-(\text{pre-test probability}))

In both (
2
and
3.1
) the presence of the covariate, cough, leads to a different post-test probability expression than the one shown in (
3.1
), which is most often seen in the standard medical diagnosis literature. The presence of the covariate cough therefore prohibits pure recall solutions. In this work, we investigate whether this problem will be challenging for ChatGPT.

3.2
Potential errors

We first list common errors that appear in probabilistic medical diagnostic reasoning, and we briefly discuss the connection of these errors with the prevailing probabilistic medical diagnosis framework. We will tally the frequency of these errors in the responses of ChatGPT.

The first error occurs when one ignores information; namely, when one arrives at a post-test probability that ignores the fact that the patient has a cough.

Error 1
.

Even though one has information about the patient’s cough, one still provides, as a post-test probability,
p(dz+|test+),
instead of
p(dz+|test+,cough).
I.e., one assumes either that
dz+\perp cough
or that the quantity of interest is
p(dz+|test+)
instead of
p(dz+|test+,cough)
.

The assumption that the quantity of interest is
p(dz+|test+)
instead of
p(dz+|test+,cough)
might occur because the running example in the medical diagnosis literature focuses on
p(dz+|test+)
. In our prompts, we will request the post-test probability that conditions on cough. So, if ChatGPT answers with (
3.1
), we will count it as Error
1
.

The next error occurs when one considers the test to be independent of patient covariates.

Error 2
.

When computing the posterior
p(dz+|test+,dz+),
one writes
p(test+|dz+)
instead of
p(test+|dz+,cough)
on the right hand side of (
2
). I.e., one assumes that
test+\perp cough.

Error
2
assumes that sensitivity does not depend on patient covariates. The assumption that sensitivity does not depend on patient covariates is criticized and thought to be generally false
(
Harrell et al. 2001
;
Moons et al. 1997
;
Moons and Harrell 2003
;
Hlatky et al. 1984
)
.
Indeed, in Covid-19, which is our focus here, symptoms affect sensitivity, as shown in
Prince-Guerra et al. 2021
, where the authors find rapid test sensitivities of 0.926 in symptomatic and 0.786 in asymptomatic patients. We would not expect ChatGPT to reference
Prince-Guerra et al. 2021
, but if it assumes independence between the test and patient symptoms, which is a false assumption in general, we will consider it an error.
Note that Error
2
can only occur if one is writing the post-test probability
p(dz+|test+,cough),
which is (
2
). I.e., if one claims to write
p(dz+|test+),
as in (
3.1
), then writing sensitivity as
p(test+|dz+)
is correct. Note that we ask ChatGPT to provide
p(dz+|test+,cough),
so any time sensitivity does not condition on cough, it is Error
2
.

The next error occurs when one does not condition on cough in the pre-test probability.
Although we prefer the pre-test probability written in (
2
), one might also write the pre-test probability as
p(dz+),
as in (
3.1
).

Error 3
.

One writes (
3.1
) instead of (
2
); hence, one writes
p(dz+)
instead of
p(dz+|cough)
as the pre-test probability.

Error
3.1
, using pre-test
p(dz+)
instead of
p(dz+|cough)
when
p(dz+)
appears in (
3.1
), is not a probability error; it is a clinical error. It is a clinical error because usually one would condition a pre-test probability on all available information (e.g., as with
Wells et al. 1998
, which gives us
p(dz+|covariates)
instead of
p(dz+)
). In our case, one would ideally define the pre-test probability as
p(dz+|cough)
, which is more patient-specific (the disease is more likely when there is a cough), and arguably easier to obtain than both
p(dz+)
and
p(cough|dz+),
which must be obtained in concert to evaluate (
3.1
).
Note that
p(covariates|dz+)
is not typically reported in studies.
It has also been noted that it is difficult to actually estimate
p(dz+),
since prevalence (or pre-test probability) is almost always conditional on something
(
Harrell et al. 2001
)
. For these reasons, providing (
3.1
) can be considered an error.

Error
3
might be induced by the focus in the prevailing literature on the concept of the unconditional “prevalence.” As with the claim that sensitivity is defined as
p(test+|dz+)
, one could also claim that prevalence is defined as
p(dz+),
in which case prevalence could only be used to compute
p(test+|dz+)
. This, however, would make it inapplicable in most clinical settings
(
Moons and Harrell 2003
)
.

Errors
1
-
3
, mentioned above, might be expected based on the medical diagnosis literature. We designate the following error, Error
4
, to apply to any other probability error besides the ones listed above.

Error 4
.

Miscellaneous probability error.

In this work, we are most concerned with Errors
1
-
3
, but we do tally the occurrences of Error
4
. We check for Error
4
in the first symbolic expression that ChatGPT gives for
p(dz|test,cough).
Sometimes, to complement this first equation, the system also provides follow up equations or lengthy numerical examples, which we do not check.

4
Methods

Before challenging ChatGPT’s to perform probabilistic medical diagnostic reasoning, we first assess whether it can correctly perform probabilistic reasoning at all.
Namely, we ask ChatGPT to give an example of using Bayes rule to compute
P(C|A,B).
Note that, as shown in the following prompt, we do so without medical terminology.

Listing 1:
Pure probability

⬇

User

Can

you

give

an

example

of

using

Bayes

rule

to

compute

posterior

probability

of

A

given

B

and

C
?

Following this investigation, we then ask ChatGPT to perform medical diagnostic reasoning, based on a prompt that simply renames the variables
A,B,C
as “Covid,” “positive Covid test” and “cough,” respectfully.
This puts the problem in a medical context, unlike the prompt in Listing
1
. This more medically relevant prompt is shown below.

Listing 2:
Medical variables

⬇

User

Can

you

give

an

example

of

using

Bayes

rule

to

compute

posterior

probability

of

Covid

given

a

positive

Covid

test

and

a

cough
?

We repeat the prompt in Listing
2
20 times and tally any errors that occur in ChatGPT’s expressions for
P(\text{Covid}|\text{test,cough})
.

In the next prompt, we slightly amend the prompt in Listing
2
by adding a request to use the “sensitivity” and “specificity” of the test, which are medical diagnosis terms. This might further encourage ChatGPT to access the medical diagnosis literature rather than the literature on pure probability. In alignment with this, rather than asking ChatGPT to give a “posterior” probability, which is a probabilistic term, we ask it for a “post-test probability, which is a medical term.

Listing 3:
Medical variables and diagnostic terminology

⬇

User

Can

you

give

an

example

of

using

Bayes

rule

to

compute

post

test

probability

in

a

patient

with

a

positive

Covid

test

and

a

cough

using

sensitivity

and

specificity

of

the

test
.

If ChatGPT neglects to take cough into account at all, i.e., if it only gives
P(Covid\mbox{-}19|test+)
, we record this as Error
1
, and then ask ChatGPT to condition on cough, and to give
p(Covid\mbox{-}19|cough,test+),
so that we can still evaluate the expression it gives for
p(Covid\mbox{-}19|cough,test+)
.

⬇

User

This

is

P
(
COVID
-19|
Positive

test
),

not

P
(
COVID
-19|
Positive

test
,

cough
)

though

isn
’
t

it
?

We repeat the prompt in Listing
3
20 times and tally the errors in ChatGPT’s expression for
p(Covid-19|test,cough).

We next develop a prompt that breaks the probabilistic medical reasoning procedure into steps and asks ChatGPT to avoid certain probabilistic assumptions that are associated with the errors in Section
3.2
. In particular, we first prompt ChatGPT as follows.

Listing 4:
Prompt engineering

⬇

User

In

the

following

conversation
,

I

will

give

you

prompts
.

Please

use

the

following

steps

to

respond
.

Step

1.

Determine

what

is

the

disease
,

what

is

the

test

result
,

and

what

is

the

covariate
.

Step

2.

Write

P
(
disease

|

test

result
,

covariates
)

using

Bayes

rule
.

Condition

on

covariates

throughout
.

Do

not

make

any

independence

assumptions
.

Write

the

denominator

using

the

law

of

total

probability
,

conditional

on

covariates
.

Step

3.

Write

the

last

formula

in

terms

of

the

disease
,

test

result
,

and

covariate

given

in

the

prompt
.

Step

4.

State

which

parts

of

the

last

formula

correspond

to

the

sensitivity

and

pre
-
test

probability
.

If

you

understand

these

steps
,

please

say

only

"
understood
".

After ChatGPT has confirmed that it has understood the steps in Listing
4
, we then repeat the prompt in Listing
3
, which is shown below for convenience.

⬇

User

Can

you

give

an

example

of

using

Bayes

rule

to

compute

post

test

in

a

patient

with

a

positive

Covid

test

and

a

cough

using

sensitivity

and

specificity

of

the

test
.

For the prompt in Listing
4
, we show an example conversation, and then we perform 20 replicates and tally the expressions that are observed.

For each conversation, we opened a new, separate session with ChatGPT. Conversations were performed through the browser interface to allow for interaction. All conversations were executed from the same user’s account, and the complete conversations are recorded in the Appendix. All conversations took place between May and July of 2023 with the standard, free, publicly available ChatGPT available at that time (version 3.5) at
https://chat.openai.com
(note that the timestamps on the conversations with links in the footnotes in Section
5
below are from November, because that is when the sharing links were created). Confidence intervals were computed using Wilson’s method.

5
Results

5.1
Pure probability

We first ask ChatGPT to compute the probability of
A
given
B
and
C
as in the prompt in Listing
1
.
We show an example conversation (for compactness here, we have used an ellipsis (…) to truncate some of ChatGPT’s response, but we include the full conversation in
A.1.1
).

We see that ChatGPT computes the posterior,
P(A|B,C)=\frac{P(B|A,C)P(A|C)}{P(B|C)},
correctly. Interestingly, even though ChatGPT is completely unprompted to give an example in a medical setting, it gives, in addition to the symbolic expression for
P(A|B,C),
a real-world medical example, which is reported in

).

Note that, in the conversations tallied in Table
1
, ChatGPT does not make any of the Errors
1
-
3
.
Note that once, ChatGPT computes the pure-probability analog to (
3.1
), in which the prior/pretest is the “unconditional”
P(A)
(in this case, for the prompt in Listing
1
, since we do not specify that this is a medical diagnosis problem, this is not technically Error
3
) (note that we say analog, because the variable names are
A,B,
and
C
instead of “Covid”, “test” and “cough”).
However, as in the example conversation above, it generally computes the pure-probability analog to (
2
), because it defines the prior/pretest to be
p(A|C)
. It makes no probability errors in the main expression for
P(A|B,C).

Medical variables

.

We see that ChatGPT makes errors about half the time. This is in contrast to the results in Table
1
, in which only Error
3
was made (and, in the context in Section
5.1
, because no medical context was given in the prompt, Error
3
was not technically an error).
The simple change in the names of the variables from
A,B,C
to “Covid”, “test”, and “cough” has changed the behavior of ChatGPT, even though the problem it is solving is essentially identical to the one it solved correctly in Section
5.1
.

5.3
Medical variables and diagnostic terminology

.

\displaystyle\text{P(COVID-19}|\text{Positive test, cough})

\displaystyle=\frac{\text{P(Positive test, cough}|\text{COVID-19) * P(COVID-19)}}{\text{P(Positive test, cough)}}.

ChatGPT has written the post-test probability to depend on

as in (

), which is Error

.

6
Discussion

In this work, we explored ChatGPT’s ability to perform probabilistic medical diagnostic reasoning.
We gave ChatGPT a problem in which there was not just a test result, but also the presence of a symptom, which was an unusual, difficult diagnostic problem. This can be challenging for language models, since they perform best when they have seen many examples of the problem they are solving in their training data. This said, ChatGPT also has access to large amounts of text on pure probability calculations.

We prompted ChatGPT to give us examples of post-test probability using terms that ranged from those in pure probability texts (Section
5.1
) to those that appear in the medical diagnosis literature (Section
5.3
). We found that the presence of medical terminology caused ChatGPT to perform poorly.
It is interesting to note that ChatGPT often considers sensitivity to be unrelated to patient covariates, which is Error
2
.
This is an error that is commonly made by humans who are using the medical diagnosis framework, which is discussed in
Moons et al. 1997
;
Harrell et al. 2001
. Large language models, because they learn from data that is generated by humans, can therefore learn not just harmful biases, as demonstrated by
Omiye et al. 2023
, but also, as we show, tendencies to make statistical errors. Possibly, our investigation might show us how to better develop educational resources on medical probabilistic reasoning. Concretely, it may be important to better integrate educational material about probability into the medical diagnosis literature. This would allow ChatGPT—and human users—to simultaneously access text on both probability and medical reasoning in the same place, which might ultimately enhance their ability to perform probabilistic medical diagnostic reasoning for nonstandard problems, such as the one we show here.

We also suspect that the terminology in the standard medical diagnostic reasoning framework takes ChatGPT to a (latent) space that is too far from where the probability question-answer examples themselves live. This causes probability errors.
The engineered prompt in Listing
4
breaks the dependence between interpreting the medical terminology and the probabilistic manipulations themselves, and we find that this improves ChatGPT’s responses, as shown in Section
5.4
. The engineered prompt also guards against erroneous independence assumptions. The procedure in Listing
4
is quite general; it could apply to a variety of disease, test, covariate scenarios;
note, however, that ChatGPT still makes a probability error when using Listing
4
, precluding the use of such a procedure for real diagnostic reasoning.

It is remarkable that ChatGPT can, with or without prompt engineering, somewhat mimic probabilistic medical diagnostic reasoning. However, we show here that medical diagnostic reasoning, which requires that one must map medical terms to probability objects, perform reasoning on the probability objects, and then map back to medical terms, might not be reliably solved by systems such as ChatGPT. Although ChatGPT is considered a marvel of modern computing, it does not appear to be able to reliably perform rule-based logical reasoning. Ironically, computer programs usually excel at rule-based logical reasoning. We suspect that this might be a structural issue with ChatGPT, in which case more “training data” might not help. It might be necessary to combine language models, such as ChatGPT, with formal, probabilistic solvers, so that the language model can interpret the user input, a rule-based solver can be called to perform symbolic math, and then the language model can translate the results back to human-readable form.

correctly

the

,

has

,

P
(

|

),

0.95

(95%).

Additionally
,

the

result

when

the

person

does

not

have

the

disease
,

P
(
Positive
|

),

(2%).

(

/

(

/

=

0.0095

/

\approx

(32.4%)

Bayes
’

is

that

about

.

and

,

we

use

Bayes
’

rule
.

Let
’
s

following

:

A
:

Having

COVID
-19

B
:

Testing

positive

for

COVID
-19

C
:

Having

a

cough

We

want

to

compute

P
(
A

|

B
,

C
),

which

represents

the

posterior

of

having

COVID
-19

given

a

positive

COVID

test

result

and

a

cough
.

’

theorem

states
:

(
A

|

B
,

C
)

=

(
P
(
B

|

A
,

C
)

*

P
(
A

|

C
))

/

P
(
B

|

C
)

To

apply

’

theorem
,

we

need

some

additional

information
:

P
(
B

|

A
,

C
):

The

of

for

COVID
-19

given

you

have

COVID
-19

and

a

cough
.

(

|

C
):

The

of

COVID
-19

cough
.

P
(
B

|

C
):

The

probability

of

testing

positive

for

COVID
-19

given

a

cough
.

,

,

.

’

|

,

)

-19

10%)

P
(
B

|

)

)

Now

’

/

COVID
-19

in

population
)

(

\cap

It writes
P(A|B,C)=\frac{(P(A)*P(B|A)*P(C|A,B))}{P(B,C)},
which translates (using the medical names it defined) to
P(COVID-19|test,Cough)
as

\frac{P(COVID-19)*P(test|COVID-19)*P(Cough|COVID-19,test)}{P(test,Cough)}.

This is a valid, although clinically unusual, expression, because it writes in a sense the “sensitivity” of“cough,” if one could think of cough as a test. It correctly identifies the real sensitivity of the test here, without making Error
2
. In this case we see that the system goes to great lengths to insert an expression that corresponds to the standard definition of sensitivity,
p(test+|dz+)
. It does still make Error
3
.

denote

:

=

.

1%

)

(

\neg

)

=

\neg

)

*

(1

-

(

(

)

represents

It makes Error
3
, since it defines
A
as Covid. It does correctly condition on cough and test result in the posterior, since it defines
B
as both of those events; however, it defines sensitivity as the “probability of a positive test result given that the patient has COVID-19,” and then it writes
P(A|B)=(P(B|A)*P(A))/P(B)
and then plugs in sensitivity

P(A|B)=\frac{(Sensitivity*P(A))}{den},
where
den=(Sensitivity*P(A)+(1-Specificity)*P(notA))
which means that
P(B|A)=p(test|covid,cough)
is considered to be
p(test|covid),
which is Error
2
.

It claims to condition on both test and cough in
P(Covid|PositiveTest,Cough).

It defines “[s]ensitivity of the Covid test: 95% (probability of a positive test given that the patient has Covid),” which is Error
2
, and it uses an unconditional pre-test,
P(covid),
which is Error
3
. It makes Error
4
by writing
p(positivetest)
within the denominator of
P(Covid|PositiveTest,Cough)=(P(PositiveTest|Covid)*P(Covid))/P(PositiveTest)
. It is possible one could arrive at this expression by assuming independence between cough and covid, and only writing this independence on the right hand side of the expression, which would be Error
1
and Error
2
, but because ChatGPT still defines the posterior to depend on cough, we instead consider it to be Error
3
and Error
4
.

))

/

(

\cap

)

/

\approx

(

Therefore
,

-
test

It correctly conditions on cough and test. In
P(A|B,C)=(P(B,C|A)*P(A))/P(B,C).
It uses an unconditional prior, which is Error
3
. However it writes

which assumes that

which is Error
